Tengo que hacer una prueba para una entrevista técnica, ayudme a entender y traducir la prueba completamente. Luego dime que es lo que necesito entregar, que es lo que están evaluando y las cosas importantes que debo tener en cuenta para ser contratado.

Esta es la prueba:

Thanks for continuing with the process. This exercise is designed to look like the real work you'd be doing in this role: you'll receive a short product background, a lightly-specified ticket, and a seed dataset — and you'll decide what to build, plan it, and implement it the way the team you'd be joining actually works: agent-first.
We care far more about senior engineering and product judgment than about completeness. A smaller, correct, well-reasoned slice beats a broad one that's half-broken.
⏱ Scope & time
Budget your effort to 4–6 hours of focused work. This is a deliberate cap, not a target to exceed. Part of what we're evaluating is how you prioritize under a real constraint.
Agent leverage doesn't raise the bar on breadth — a correct, well-reasoned slice still beats a broad one. If your agents make you fast, spend the surplus on verification and edge cases, not more features.
If you run out of time, stop and document what you'd do next in the README rather than rushing a broken feature.
🤖 AI use
We expect you to work agent-first — this is how the team you'd join works. Engineers on this team pick up a ticket, build an AI-assisted implementation plan, and implement with AI coding agents (Claude Code, Copilot, Cursor, etc.) by default. We are not testing whether you can write code without AI. Doing this challenge without AI tooling is not a bonus — it tells us nothing we need to know. What we're interested in is how you direct, review, and verify agent output.
Because of that, two artifacts are required deliverables (details under "What to submit"):
An implementation plan (PLAN.md) written before you implement. Raw and unpolished is fine — better, even. We expect the plan to visibly precede the bulk of implementation in your AI log (planning conversations early, code generation after). We expect to see you plan with (or before) the agent, not just generate code.
An AI interaction log with as much raw detail as possible: actual prompts and model responses, where you accepted, rejected, or redirected the AI and why, and a short reflection naming specific moments where you caught the AI being wrong or chose to override it. Please also include one honest line on which tools/models you used and for what (authoring vs. reviewing vs. not at all). We don't score which tools you used — we look at whether the division of labor was deliberate. A missing, sanitized, or obviously one-shot AI log is a serious negative signal — messier and honest beats tidy.
Optional, but strong signal: if you write agent-context artifacts for this task — a CLAUDE.md, .cursorrules, custom commands/skills, a system prompt — commit them. Reusable agent instructions are exactly the kind of thing this team builds.
🏢 Product background
Relay is a fictional B2B SaaS that helps service businesses track inbound customer activity — calls, leads, appointments — across their locations. Customers range from single-site shops to groups with 15+ locations. Each customer gets a reporting dashboard: today it shows raw totals per location and not much else.
Two things the team keeps hearing:
Account managers say customers ask "is this number normal for us?" and can't answer it from the dashboard.
The support team says customers with multiple locations struggle to spot which location needs attention.
The team works spec-driven and AI-first: engineers get tickets like the one below, build a plan, and implement with AI agents by default.
🎫 The ticket
DASH-247 — Help customers understand whether their recent activity is normal
Customers looking at their dashboard can't tell whether this week's numbers are good, bad, or typical for them. Product wants the dashboard to help a customer answer "is this normal for us?" at a glance, without exporting data or calling their account manager.
Notes from product: probably involves comparing against some baseline, multi-location customers matter, a customer admin should be able to look at this Monday morning and act on it.
Out of scope per product: alerting/notifications, ML/forecasting.
The ticket is deliberately light — that's realistic, not an oversight. Deciding what it actually means (what "normal" is, what window, what view) is part of the exercise. Your interpretation and scoping decisions are evaluated criteria, not just tolerated variance.
Questions during the challenge? Email them via the recruiter — we treat good questions as signal, not weakness. If you'd rather not wait on an answer, document the question and the working assumption you proceeded on in PLAN.md. Either path is fine; silently guessing is the only wrong one.
✅ What to build
Your interpretation of the ticket is yours to make and defend, but every submission must contain this skeleton:
Backend: at least one API endpoint that performs real aggregation over the seed data (not pass-through queries), serving your dashboard feature.
Frontend: an SPA view that consumes it, with at least one piece of user-controlled input (filter, date range, location selector, toggle — your call) whose state survives a page reload.
Tests that run, with a one-line note in the README on how to run them.
A relational database with a real schema and migrations (your stack's standard tooling), loaded from the provided seed data.
Stack (required): build this in .NET 8+ (C#) on the backend and Angular on the frontend. This is the stack the team you'd be joining works in, and the exercise is evaluated in it — submissions in another stack can't be evaluated for this role. The database is flexible: any relational DB is fine (SQL Server is what the team runs, so it's a nice touch — but don't burn your time budget on DB setup). Tell us in the README about the notable choices you made within the stack (ORM vs. raw SQL, state management approach, etc.) and why.
Don't invent your own dataset — use the seed data provided (below) so your time goes into the feature, not data modeling. Handle the unglamorous parts of real data honestly: empty ranges, odd values, aggregate correctness.
🌱 Seed data
Schema + seed dataset (two tables: accounts and activity_events) are provided in a starter repository:
**https://github.com/Qualitara/tv-analytics-takehome**
Clone it into a fresh repository of your own (please don't fork — forks are publicly linked to the source repo). If you can't access GitHub for any reason, reply to the recruiter and we'll get you the files directly.
🚫 Explicitly out of scope
Skip these so they don't eat your budget. We won't penalize their absence:
Auth / login — assume a single trusted customer admin; a hardcoded identity is fine.
Alerting / notifications and ML / forecasting — per the ticket.
Production infra — no Kubernetes or CI required. Local run is fine.
Visual polish — function over form. Unstyled-but-clear beats pretty-but-broken.
📦 What to submit
Reply directly to the recruiter's email with a link to a public GitHub repository containing:
The code — backend, frontend, and migrations, in a repo we can clone and run.
README.md — how to run it locally (DB setup, env vars, commands); your interpretation of the ticket; key assumptions and design decisions with the trade-offs you weighed; what you deliberately deferred and why; what you'd do with another day.
PLAN.md — the implementation plan you built from the ticket before coding. Leave it as-written; don't retrofit it to match the final code.
AI interaction log (ai-log/ directory or AI_LOG.md) — as described above, plus any committed agent-context artifacts.
Tests that run.
Please make sure the repo is public and that the README, PLAN.md, and AI log are committed before you reply.
🔎 How we evaluate
At a high level, we're looking at:
Product & spec judgment — how you interpreted an underspecified ticket: what you asked or assumed, what you chose to build and not build, and why.
Agentic working discipline — a plan that genuinely preceded the code, agent direction with precise context, and evidence you caught and corrected agent mistakes rather than rubber-stamping output.
Correctness & engineering judgment — does it work, and are the decisions sound for a senior engineer? Aggregates that are actually right against the seed data matter more than feature count.
Data handling — SQL/aggregation reasoning, and honest treatment of messy real-world data.
Test quality — meaningful tests on the parts that matter, not coverage farming.
Communication — a README and plan that let us reconstruct your reasoning without you in the room.
We don't expect perfection in 4–6 hours. We expect to see how a senior, agent-first engineer reasons, prioritizes, and ships a correct slice.
Good luck — we're looking forward to seeing how you think.

# 📄 Traducción completa de la prueba

> Gracias por continuar con el proceso. Este ejercicio está diseñado para parecerse al trabajo real que harías en este rol: recibirás un contexto de producto breve, un ticket ligeramente especificado y un dataset semilla — y tú decidirás qué construir, lo planificarás y lo implementarás de la forma en que realmente trabaja el equipo al que te unirías: **agent-first** (con agentes de IA por defecto).
>
> Nos importa **mucho más el criterio senior de ingeniería y de producto que la completitud**. Una porción pequeña, correcta y bien razonada le gana a una amplia y a medio funcionar.

## ⏱ Alcance y tiempo

> Presupuesta tu esfuerzo en **4–6 horas de trabajo enfocado**. Es un tope deliberado, no una meta a superar. Parte de lo que evaluamos es cómo priorizas bajo una restricción real.
>
> El apalancamiento con agentes **no sube el listón de amplitud** — una porción correcta y bien razonada sigue ganando a una amplia. Si tus agentes te hacen rápido, gasta el excedente en **verificación y casos borde**, no en más features.
>
> Si te quedas sin tiempo, detente y documenta en el README qué harías a continuación, en lugar de apurar una funcionalidad rota.

## 🤖 Uso de IA

> Esperamos que trabajes **agent-first** — así trabaja el equipo al que te unirías. Los ingenieros de este equipo toman un ticket, construyen un plan de implementación asistido por IA, e implementan con agentes de codificación IA (Claude Code, Copilot, Cursor, etc.) por defecto. **No estamos evaluando si puedes escribir código sin IA.** Hacer este reto sin herramientas de IA no es un bonus — no nos dice nada que necesitemos saber. Lo que nos interesa es **cómo diriges, revisas y verificas** la salida del agente.
>
> Por eso, dos artefactos son **entregables obligatorios**:
>
> 1. Un **plan de implementación (PLAN.md)** escrito *antes* de implementar. Crudo y sin pulir está bien — mejor, incluso. Esperamos que el plan **preceda visiblemente** al grueso de la implementación en tu log de IA (conversaciones de planificación primero, generación de código después). Esperamos verte planificar *con* (o *antes* de) el agente, no solo generar código.
> 2. Un **log de interacción con IA** con el mayor detalle crudo posible: prompts reales y respuestas del modelo, dónde aceptaste, rechazaste o redirigiste a la IA y por qué, y una **reflexión corta nombrando momentos específicos donde detectaste que la IA se equivocaba o decidiste anularla**. Incluye también **una línea honesta** sobre qué herramientas/modelos usaste y para qué (autoría vs. revisión vs. nada). No puntuamos qué herramientas usaste — miramos si la división del trabajo fue deliberada. **Un log de IA faltante, saneado o evidentemente de un solo tiro es una señal negativa seria; desordenado y honesto le gana a ordenado.**
>
> Opcional, pero **señal fuerte**: si escribes artefactos de contexto para agentes en esta tarea — un `CLAUDE.md`, `.cursorrules`, comandos/skills personalizados, un system prompt — **commitéalos**. Las instrucciones reutilizables para agentes son exactamente el tipo de cosa que este equipo construye.

## 🏢 Contexto de producto

> **Relay** es un SaaS B2B ficticio que ayuda a negocios de servicios a rastrear actividad entrante de clientes — llamadas, leads, citas — a través de sus sedes/ubicaciones. Los clientes van desde locales únicos hasta grupos con más de 15 ubicaciones. Cada cliente tiene un dashboard de reportes: hoy muestra totales crudos por ubicación y poco más.
>
> Dos cosas que el equipo escucha constantemente:
> - Los *account managers* dicen que los clientes preguntan **"¿este número es normal para nosotros?"** y no pueden responderlo desde el dashboard.
> - El equipo de soporte dice que los clientes con **múltiples ubicaciones** batallan para detectar **qué ubicación necesita atención**.
>
> El equipo trabaja *spec-driven* y *AI-first*: los ingenieros reciben tickets como el de abajo, arman un plan e implementan con agentes por defecto.

## 🎫 El ticket

> **DASH-247 — Ayudar a los clientes a entender si su actividad reciente es normal**
>
> Los clientes que miran su dashboard no pueden saber si los números de esta semana son buenos, malos o típicos para ellos. Producto quiere que el dashboard ayude al cliente a responder **"¿esto es normal para nosotros?"** de un vistazo, sin exportar datos ni llamar a su account manager.
>
> *Notas de producto:* probablemente implique **comparar contra alguna línea base**; los clientes **multi-ubicación importan**; un **admin del cliente debería poder mirar esto un lunes por la mañana y actuar**.
>
> *Fuera de alcance según producto:* alertas/notificaciones, ML/forecasting.
>
> El ticket es deliberadamente ligero — eso es realista, no un descuido. **Decidir qué significa realmente** (qué es "normal", qué ventana de tiempo, qué vista) es parte del ejercicio. **Tu interpretación y tus decisiones de alcance son criterios de evaluación**, no solo variación tolerada.
>
> ¿Preguntas durante el reto? Envíalas por email vía el reclutador — tratamos las buenas preguntas como señal, no como debilidad. Si prefieres no esperar respuesta, **documenta la pregunta y el supuesto de trabajo bajo el que procediste en PLAN.md**. Cualquiera de los dos caminos está bien; **adivinar en silencio es el único camino equivocado**.

## ✅ Qué construir

> Tu interpretación del ticket es tuya para hacerla y defenderla, pero **toda entrega debe contener este esqueleto**:
>
> - **Backend:** al menos un endpoint de API que haga **agregación real** sobre los datos semilla (no consultas pasa-through), sirviendo tu feature del dashboard.
> - **Frontend:** una vista SPA que lo consuma, con **al menos una entrada controlada por el usuario** (filtro, rango de fechas, selector de ubicación, toggle — tú decides) **cuyo estado sobreviva a un recargado de página**.
> - **Tests que corran**, con una nota de una línea en el README sobre cómo ejecutarlos.
> - **Una base de datos relacional** con esquema real y **migraciones** (herramientas estándar de tu stack), cargada desde los datos semilla provistos.
>
> **Stack (obligatorio):** constrúyelo en **.NET 8+ (C#)** en el backend y **Angular** en el frontend. Este es el stack en el que trabaja el equipo, y el ejercicio se evalúa en él — **entregas en otro stack no pueden evaluarse para este rol**. La base de datos es flexible: cualquier BD relacional está bien (**SQL Server** es lo que corre el equipo, así que es un detalle agradable — pero no quemes tu presupuesto de tiempo en montar la BD). Cuéntanos en el README las **decisiones notables** que tomaste dentro del stack (ORM vs. SQL crudo, enfoque de manejo de estado, etc.) y por qué.
>
> **No inventes tu propio dataset** — usa los datos semilla provistos (abajo) para que tu tiempo vaya a la feature, no al modelado de datos. Maneja honestamente las partes poco glamorosas de los datos reales: **rangos vacíos, valores raros, corrección de agregados**.

## 🌱 Datos semilla

> Esquema + dataset semilla (dos tablas: `accounts` y `activity_events`) están en un repo starter:
> **https://github.com/Qualitara/tv-analytics-takehome**
>
> **Clónalo en un repositorio nuevo tuyo (por favor no hagas fork** — los forks quedan públicamente vinculados al repo origen). Si no puedes acceder a GitHub, responde al reclutador y te pasamos los archivos directamente.

## 🚫 Explícitamente fuera de alcance

> Sáltate esto para que no te coma el presupuesto. No penalizaremos su ausencia:
> - **Auth / login** — asume un único admin de cliente confiable; una identidad hardcodeada está bien.
> - **Alertas / notificaciones** y **ML / forecasting** — según el ticket.
> - **Infraestructura de producción** — no se requiere Kubernetes ni CI. Correrlo local está bien.
> - **Pulido visual** — función sobre forma. **Sin estilos pero claro le gana a bonito pero roto.**

## 📦 Qué entregar

> Responde directamente al email del reclutador con un enlace a un **repositorio público de GitHub** que contenga:
>
> - **El código** — backend, frontend y migraciones, en un repo que podamos clonar y correr.
> - **README.md** — cómo correrlo localmente (setup de BD, variables de entorno, comandos); tu interpretación del ticket; supuestos clave y decisiones de diseño **con los trade-offs que sopesaste**; qué diferiste deliberadamente y por qué; qué harías con **un día más**.
> - **PLAN.md** — el plan de implementación que construiste desde el ticket **antes de codificar**. Déjalo tal como fue escrito; **no lo retrofitees** para que coincida con el código final.
> - **Log de interacción con IA** (directorio `ai-log/` o `AI_LOG.md`) — como se describió arriba, más cualquier artefacto de contexto para agentes que hayas commiteado.
> - **Tests que corran.**
>
> Asegúrate de que el repo sea **público** y de que README, PLAN.md y el log de IA estén **commiteados** antes de responder.

## 🔎 Cómo evaluamos

> A alto nivel, miramos:
>
> - **Criterio de producto y especificación** — cómo interpretaste un ticket subespecificado: qué preguntaste o asumiste, qué elegiste construir y no construir, y por qué.
> - **Disciplina de trabajo agéntico** — un plan que genuinamente precedió al código, dirección del agente con contexto preciso, y evidencia de que **detectaste y corregiste errores del agente en lugar de sellar su salida**.
> - **Corrección y criterio de ingeniería** — ¿funciona?, ¿son sólidas las decisiones para un ingeniero senior? **Agregados que sean realmente correctos contra los datos semilla importan más que la cantidad de features.**
> - **Manejo de datos** — razonamiento SQL/agregación y tratamiento honesto de datos reales sucios.
> - **Calidad de tests** — tests significativos en las partes que importan, no *coverage farming*.
> - **Comunicación** — un README y un plan que nos permitan **reconstruir tu razonamiento sin ti en la sala**.
>
> No esperamos perfección en 4–6 horas. Esperamos ver cómo un ingeniero senior *agent-first* razona, prioriza y entrega una porción correcta.

---

# 🎯 Qué necesitas entregar (checklist literal)

| # | Entregable | Obligatorio | Nota |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Repo **público** de GitHub (clonado, **NO fork**) | ✅ | |
| 2 | Backend .NET 8+ con ≥1 endpoint de **agregación real** | ✅ | Nada de `SELECT *` pasa-through |
| 3 | Frontend **Angular** SPA consumiendo el endpoint | ✅ | |
| 4 | ≥1 input de usuario cuyo **estado sobreviva al reload** | ✅ | Query params en URL o localStorage |
| 5 | BD relacional con **esquema + migraciones** | ✅ | SQL Server suma puntos |
| 6 | Carga desde el **seed data provisto** | ✅ | No inventes datos |
| 7 | **Tests que corran** + cómo correrlos (1 línea en README) | ✅ | |
| 8 | **PLAN.md** escrito antes de codificar, sin retocar | ✅ | |
| 9 | **README.md** completo (ver estructura abajo) | ✅ | |
| 10 | **AI_LOG.md** o `ai-log/` crudo y honesto | ✅ | |
| 11 | `CLAUDE.md` / `.cursorrules` / skills / comandos | ⭐ Opcional pero fuerte | |

---

# 🧠 Qué están evaluando realmente

Lo dicen entre líneas y hay que leerlo bien. Esta prueba **no** es sobre construir un dashboard. Es sobre tres cosas:

### 1. ¿Piensas como producto, no solo como programador?
El ticket es vago **a propósito**. Van a comparar tu interpretación contra la de otros candidatos. Lo que califican no es "elegiste bien", es **si defendiste tu elección con razonamiento explícito y trade-offs**. "Adivinar en silencio es el único camino equivocado" es la frase más importante de todo el documento.

### 2. ¿Sabes dirigir agentes, o solo copias/pegas lo que escupen?
El diferenciador es **la evidencia de que atrapaste a la IA equivocándose**. Un log limpio y perfecto es señal *negativa*. Ellos quieren ver: prompt → respuesta del modelo → "esto está mal porque X" → redirección → resultado. Sin al menos 3–5 momentos así, tu entrega se lee como *rubber-stamping*.

### 3. ¿Tus números son correctos?
"Aggregates that are actually right against the seed data matter more than feature count." El seed data **tiene trampas plantadas** (casi con seguridad: eventos duplicados, ubicaciones sin actividad, rangos vacíos, timestamps raros, valores nulos, semanas parciales). Detectarlas y manejarlas explícitamente vale más que cualquier feature adicional.

---

# ⚠️ Lo importante para ser contratado

## Las trampas (dónde se cae la gente)

1. **Hacer demasiado.** El texto lo repite tres veces. Si entregas 6 features a medias, te descartan. Una vista, un endpoint, correcta al 100%, con edge cases cubiertos → contratado.
2. **PLAN.md retrofiteado.** Van a mirar el **historial de git y los timestamps del AI log**. Si tu primer commit es el código y luego "add PLAN.md", es una bandera roja. → **Commitea PLAN.md primero, en su propio commit, antes de cualquier línea de código.**
3. **AI log saneado.** "Missing, sanitized, or obviously one-shot AI log is a serious negative signal." No lo edites para que se vea bien. Guarda las transcripciones crudas.
4. **Ignorar "state survives reload".** Es un requisito literal y fácil de olvidar. La forma senior: **sincronizar filtros con query params de la URL** (`/dashboard?from=...&to=...&locationId=...`) — es compartible/bookmarkeable, no solo localStorage. Menciona ese razonamiento en el README.
5. **Gastar tiempo en CSS.** Explícitamente no lo puntúan.
6. **Cambiar de stack.** .NET 8+ y Angular son no-negociables.

## Las palancas de alto retorno

- **Interpretación fuerte y defendida del "normal".** Sin ML: baseline = promedio móvil de las N semanas previas *comparando mismo día de la semana* (el tráfico de un negocio de servicios es fuertemente estacional por día). Delta % + una banda de "rango típico". Eso es defendible y explicable a un admin un lunes por la mañana.
- **Resolver explícitamente el segundo dolor:** una vista multi-ubicación **ordenada por desviación respecto a su propia línea base**, no por volumen absoluto. Eso responde literal a "qué ubicación necesita atención" y demuestra que leíste el contexto de producto, no solo el ticket.
- **Tests sobre la aritmética de agregación con fixtures conocidos** (baseline sin datos, ubicación con 1 solo día de historia, división por cero, rango vacío, semana parcial). Ahí es donde "test quality, not coverage farming" se demuestra.
- **Una sección de "Preguntas abiertas y supuestos" en PLAN.md** con 4–6 preguntas reales que le harías a producto y el supuesto bajo el que procediste en cada una. Esto ataca directamente su criterio #1 y es barato de escribir.
- **Commitear un `CLAUDE.md`** con reglas del proyecto para el agente. Dicen que es "exactamente el tipo de cosa que este equipo construye" — es el multiplicador opcional más barato del ejercicio.
- **README con "qué diferí y por qué" + "qué haría con un día más".** Convierte tus recortes de alcance de debilidad en evidencia de criterio.

## Presupuesto sugerido (4–6h)

| Bloque | Tiempo | Qué |
|---|---|---|
| Exploración del seed data + PLAN.md | ~1h | Perfila los datos *antes* de planear; encuentra las trampas. Commit de PLAN.md. |
| Backend: esquema, migraciones, seed, endpoint de agregación | ~1.5–2h | |
| Frontend Angular: una vista + filtros en URL | ~1–1.5h | |
| Tests + edge cases | ~45min | |
| README + limpieza del AI log | ~30–45min | No lo dejes para los últimos 5 minutos |

---

¿Quieres que sigamos? Puedo ayudarte a: clonar el starter y **perfilar el seed data** para encontrar las anomalías plantadas, redactar el **PLAN.md** contigo (y commitearlo primero, con timestamp limpio), o montar el esqueleto .NET 8 + Angular. Dime por dónde arrancamos.

{
  "file_path": "c:\\repos\\personal\\qualitara\\home-challenge\\tools\\export-ai-log.ps1",
  "content": "<#\n.SYNOPSIS\n  Exports raw Claude Code session transcripts (.jsonl) to readable Markdown.\n\n.DESCRIPTION\n  Claude Code stores every session verbatim as JSONL under\n  ~/.claude/projects/<slugified-cwd>/<session-id>.jsonl\n  This script renders those to Markdown so the raw log is reviewable\n  without a JSON viewer. Both the .jsonl and the .md are committed:\n  the .jsonl is the unedited source of truth, the .md is for humans.\n\n  Nothing is edited or omitted except that oversized tool results are\n  truncated (and explicitly marked as such).\n\n.EXAMPLE\n  ./tools/export-ai-log.ps1\n  ./tools/export-ai-log.ps1 -MaxToolResultChars 4000\n#>\n[CmdletBinding()]\nparam(\n    # Directory holding the session .jsonl files. Defaults to this repo's project dir.\n    [string]$SessionDir = \"$env:USERPROFILE\\.claude\\projects\\c--repos-personal-qualitara-home-challenge\",\n\n    # Where to write the exports.\n    [string]$OutDir = (Join-Path $PSScriptRoot '..\\ai-log\\raw'),\n\n    # Tool results longer than this are truncated in the Markdown render.\n    [int]$MaxToolResultChars = 2000\n)\n\n$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'\n\nif (-not (Test-Path $SessionDir)) { throw \"Session dir not found: $SessionDir\" }\nif (-not (Test-Path $OutDir)) { New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $OutDir | Out-Null }\n$OutDir = (Resolve-Path $OutDir).Path\n\nfunction Format-Block {\n    param($block)\n\n    switch ($block.type) {\n        'text' { return $block.text }\n\n        'thinking' {\n            return \"<details><summary>[model reasoning]</summary>`n`n``````\" +\n                   \"`n$($block.thinking)`n```````n`n</details>\"\n        }\n\n        'tool_use' {\n            $json = $block.input | ConvertTo-Json -Depth 6 -Compress:$false\n            return \"**-> tool call: ``$($block.name)``**`n`n``````json`n$json`n``````\"\n        }\n\n        'tool_result' {\n            $c = $block.content\n            if ($c -is [array]) { $c = ($c | ForEach-Object { $_.text }) -join \"`n\" }\n            $c = [string]$c\n            $note = ''\n            if ($c.Length -gt $MaxToolResultChars) {\n                $note = \"`n... [truncated by export-ai-log.ps1: $($c.Length) chars total]\"\n                $c = $c.Substring(0, $MaxToolResultChars)\n            }\n            return \"**<- tool result**`n`n``````text`n$c$note`n``````\"\n        }\n\n        default { return \"_[unrendered block type: $($block.type)]_\" }\n    }\n}\n\nGet-ChildItem -Path $SessionDir -Filter *.jsonl | ForEach-Object {\n    $sessionFile = $_\n    $lines = Get-Content -LiteralPath $sessionFile.FullName\n\n    # Keep the unedited source next to the render.\n    Copy-Item -LiteralPath $sessionFile.FullName -Destination (Join-Path $OutDir $sessionFile.Name) -Force\n\n    $sb = [System.Text.StringBuilder]::new()\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine(\"# Raw session transcript — ``$($sessionFile.BaseName)``\")\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine()\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine(\"Verbatim export of a Claude Code session. Source of truth: ``$($sessionFile.Name)`` (committed alongside).\")\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine(\"Rendered by ``tools/export-ai-log.ps1``. Unedited except for truncation of long tool results, which is marked inline.\")\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine()\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine('---')\n    [void]$sb.AppendLine()\n\n    foreach ($line in $lines) {\n        if ([string]::IsNullOrWhiteSpace($line)) { continue }\n        try { $e = $line | ConvertFrom-Json } catch { continue }\n        if ($e.type -notin @('user', 'assistant')) { continue }\n\n        $role = $e.message.role\n        $ts = $e.timestamp\n        $content = $e.message.content\n\n        $rendered =\n            if ($content -is [string]) { $content }\n            else { ($content | ForEach-Object { Format-Block $_ }) -join \"`n`n\" }\n\n        if ([string]::IsNullOrWhiteSpace($rendered)) { continue }\n\n        $label = if ($role -eq 'user') { 'USER' } else { 'ASSISTANT' }\n        [void]$sb.AppendLine(\"## $label  <sub>$ts</sub>\")\n        [void]$sb.AppendLine()\n        [void]$sb.AppendLine($rendered)\n        [void]$sb.AppendLine()\n        [void]$sb.AppendLine('---')\n        [void]$sb.AppendLine()\n    }\n\n    $outFile = Join-Path $OutDir \"$($sessionFile.BaseName).md\"\n    [System.IO.File]::WriteAllText($outFile, $sb.ToString(), [System.Text.UTF8Encoding]::new($false))\n    Write-Host \"Exported $($lines.Count) events -> $outFile\"\n}\n"
}

I reviewed every diff before committing.\n- _(update this list as other tools enter the picture — or state plainly that none did.)_\n\n## Structure\n\n| Path | What it is |\n|---|---|\n| `raw/*.jsonl` | Unedited Claude Code session transcripts, straight off disk. Source of truth. |\n| `raw/*.md` | Human-readable render of the same sessions, generated by `tools/export-ai-log.ps1`. |\n| `decisions/` | Short, in-the-moment entries: what I asked for, what came back, what I did about it and why. |\n| `reflection.md` | Written at the end: the specific moments I caught the agent being wrong or overrode it. |\n\n`raw/` is machine-produced and complete. `decisions/` is where my judgment lives — the\ntranscript shows *what happened*, the decision entries show *why I let it happen or stopped it*.\n\n## Regenerating the raw log\n\n```powershell\n./tools/export-ai-log.ps1\n```\n\nReads `~/.claude/projects/<project>/*.jsonl`, copies each session verbatim into `raw/`,\nand renders a Markdown version beside it. Nothing is edited or dropped; tool results longer\nthan ~2000 chars are truncated in the Markdown render only, and the truncation is marked\ninline. ```
home-challenge/
├── tools/export-ai-log.ps1              # exporta el transcript crudo (reejecutable)

    ├── README.md                        # índice + línea honesta de herramientas/modelos
    ├── raw/
    │   ├── 41596534-….jsonl              # transcript sin editar (fuente de verdad)
    │   └── 41596534-….md                 # render legible, 34 KB
    └── decisions/
        └── 01-reading-the-brief.md      # qué pedí, qué volvió, qué acepté/corregí
```

**Cómo funciona el flujo:** `raw/` es automático y completo — corres el script cuando quieras y se actualiza solo. Tú nunca lo editas. `decisions/` es donde vive tu criterio: una entrada corta por hito (planificación, esquema de BD, endpoint, frontend, tests), escrita **en el momento**, no al final. Eso separa el *qué pasó* (verificable) del *por qué lo permití o lo frené* (lo que evalúan).

Ya tienes la primera evidencia real de "caught the AI being wrong" — la fabricación que acabo de corregir — y es genuina, no manufacturada. Ese es exactamente el tipo de momento que el brief pide en la reflexión final.

**Dos cosas pendientes que te dejo señaladas:**

1. `ai-log/reflection.md` se escribe al final, no ahora. Necesita 3–5 momentos reales; tenemos 1.
2. Vuelve a correr `./tools/export-ai-log.ps1` antes de entregar, para que `raw/` incluya las sesiones completas.

**Siguiente paso — decisión tuya:** ¿este directorio (`home-challenge`) es el repo que vas a entregar, o prefieres clonar primero el starter de Qualitara en una carpeta nueva y mover esto adentro? Lo pregunto porque el orden de commits es parte de lo que evalúan: lo correcto es `git init` → commit del `ai-log/` inicial → contenido del starter → **`PLAN.md` en su propio commit** → recién ahí el código. Si me dices cuál prefieres, lo dejo montado y seguimos con la exploración del seed data.

No, no es requerida. Es algo que agregué yo.

El enunciado solo exige: código, `README.md`, `PLAN.md`, el log de IA (`ai-log/` o `AI_LOG.md`) y tests. `tools/` no aparece.

**Por qué lo escribí:** sin script, la única forma de meter el transcript crudo en el repo es copiar el `.jsonl` a mano cada vez — y como vamos a regenerarlo varias veces durante las 4–6h, se vuelve tedioso y fácil de olvidar. El script también documenta *cómo* se produjo el log (qué se trunca y qué no), lo cual refuerza que no está editado.

**Beneficio secundario:** el brief dice que commitear artefactos de contexto para agentes es "señal fuerte". Un exportador de logs no es exactamente un `CLAUDE.md`, pero va en la misma dirección: tooling alrededor del flujo con agentes.

Dicho eso, entiendo que ensucia la raíz del repo. Mi recomendación: **moverlo dentro de `ai-log/`**, así el log queda autocontenido — el script vive junto a lo que produce, la raíz queda limpia, y sigue estando en la ruta que el revisor sí va a abrir:

```
ai-log/
├── README.md
├── export-ai-log.ps1     ← aquí
├── raw/
└── decisions/
```

¿Lo muevo así, o prefieres eliminarlo y copiar el `.jsonl` a mano?

2	
3	Seed data and context for the take-home exercise. **Clone this repository into a fresh repo of your own** (please don't fork), then follow the instructions in the take-home prompt you received by email.

21	- Use this dataset as-is — don't regenerate, extend, or replace it. Treat it like production data: it's real-world messy, not idealized.
22	
23	Questions? Route them through the recruiter, per the prompt.

1	# Relay — Product Background
2	
3	**Relay** is a B2B SaaS that helps service businesses track inbound customer activity — calls, leads, appointments — across their locations. Customers range from single-site shops to groups with 15+ locations.
4	
5	Each customer gets a **reporting dashboard**. Today it shows raw totals per location and not much else.
6	
7	Two things the team keeps hearing:
8	
9	- **Account managers** say customers ask *"is this number normal for us?"* and can't answer it from the dashboard.
10	- **The support team** says customers with multiple locations struggle to spot which location needs attention.
11	
12	## How the team works
13	
14	The engineering team works **spec-driven and AI-first**: engineers pick up tickets like the one in `TICKET.md`, build an AI-assisted implementation plan, and implement with AI coding agents by default. Judgment concentrates on planning, spec interpretation, and reviewing/hardening agent output.
15	
16	## Data model
17	
18	Two tables (see `schema.sql`):
19	
20	- **`accounts`** — one row per customer. Includes the account's IANA `timezone`.
21	- **`activity_events`** — inbound activity: `call_received`, `lead_created`, `appointment_set`. Each event has a `location` (the account's site/branch), an `occurred_at` timestamp **in UTC**, an optional `duration_seconds` (calls), and an optional `outcome`.
22	
```

## ASSISTANT  <sub>08/13/2026 22:35:08</sub>

Commit `247a342` subido. Y el `generate_seed.py` valió cada segundo — **está todo declarado en el código**. Esto es lo que plantaron:

| # | Anomalía | Dónde | Qué te obliga a decidir |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Cuenta sin ningún evento** — Quiet Harbor Spa (id 20), `rate=0.0` | `ACCOUNTS[19]` | Rango vacío, división por cero, cuenta sin baseline posible |
| 2 | **Día burst: +800 eventos** en Metro Collision (id 6) el **2026-06-03**, repartidos en 15 sedes | `BURST_*` | Un outlier que envenena cualquier media. ¿Mediana? ¿Winsorizing? |
| 3 | **12 duplicados exactos** (mismos valores, `id` nuevo) | `N_DUPLICATES` | ¿Deduplicas o no? Hay que justificarlo |
| 4 | **NULLs plantados**: 4% en `duration_seconds`, 3% en `outcome` | `NULL_*_RATE` | `AVG` ignora NULL silenciosamente; los conteos no |
| 5 | **Fines de semana ×0.35** | `if day.weekday() >= 5` | Confirma que el baseline debe comparar **mismo día de la semana** |

**Y tres cosas que no están en la tabla pero pesan más:**

**a) El dataset termina el 2026-07-28. Hoy es 2026-08-13.** Si el dashboard usa "hoy" como ancla, el cliente ve una pantalla vacía. Tienes que anclar "esta semana" al máximo `occurred_at` del dataset y decirlo en el README — es exactamente el tipo de manejo honesto de datos que evalúan.

**b) Timezones — la trampa técnica más grande.** Los eventos se generan con hora UTC centrada en las 16:00 (`gauss(16, 4)`, recortada a 0–23). Para Los Ángeles (UTC−7), un evento a la 01:00 UTC pertenece a las 18:00 del **día anterior** en hora local. Si agrupas por día en UTC, los totales diarios del cliente están mal. Hay que convertir a la zona IANA de la cuenta **antes** de agrupar — y como el rango cubre el cambio de horario de marzo, un offset fijo tampoco sirve. Phoenix además no tiene DST. Resolver esto bien es probablemente el diferenciador más fuerte de toda la prueba.

**c) Cuentas de bajo volumen** (Old Town Barbers 1.8 eventos/día, Willow Creek 2.0): un "−40% esta semana" sobre 9 eventos no significa nada. La varianza está declarada: `gauss(rate, rate*0.45)` — o sea ~45% de desviación estándar es *normal por construcción*. Eso te da la justificación cuantitativa para una banda de "rango típico" sin tocar ML.

  "content": "\"\"\"Follow-up profiling: fixes the CV bug and tests two design hypotheses.\"\"\"\nimport sqlite3, statistics\nfrom datetime import date, timedelta\n\nREPO = r\"c:\\repos\\personal\\qualitara\\home-challenge\"\ncon = sqlite3.connect(\":memory:\")\ncon.executescript(open(f\"{REPO}/schema.sql\", encoding=\"utf-8\").read())\ncon.executescript(open(f\"{REPO}/seed.sql\", encoding=\"utf-8\").read())\nq = lambda sql, *a: con.execute(sql, a).fetchall()\n\nDAY0, DAYN = date(2026, 2, 1), date(2026, 7, 27)\nALL_DAYS = [DAY0 + timedelta(d) for d in range((DAYN - DAY0).days + 1)]\nDOW = \"Mon Tue Wed Thu Fri Sat Sun\".split()\n\n\ndef h(t): print(f\"\\n{'='*78}\\n{t}\\n{'='*78}\")\n\n\nh(\"A. CALENDAR ANCHORS  (does the dataset end on a Monday?)\")\nprint(f\"   first day  {DAY0}  = {DOW[DAY0.weekday()]}\")\nprint(f\"   last  day  {DAYN}  = {DOW[DAYN.weekday()]}\")\nprint(f\"   burst day  2026-06-03 = {DOW[date(2026,6,3).weekday()]}\")\nlast_mon = DAYN - timedelta(days=DAYN.weekday())\nprint(f\"   -> last COMPLETE Mon-Sun week: {last_mon - timedelta(7)} .. {last_mon - timedelta(1)}\")\n\nh(\"B. CV, COMPUTED HONESTLY  (zero-days included in BOTH mean and variance)\")\nprint(f\"   {'id':<3} {'name':<26} {'n':<6} {'mean':<6} {'median':<7} {'stdev':<7} {'CV':<6} {'p95':<5} zero-days\")\nfor aid, name in q(\"SELECT id, name FROM accounts ORDER BY id\"):\n    counts = dict(q(\"\"\"SELECT date(occurred_at), COUNT(*) FROM activity_events\n                       WHERE account_id=? GROUP BY 1\"\"\", aid))\n    series = [counts.get(d.isoformat(), 0) for d in ALL_DAYS]   # gaps filled with 0\n    n = sum(series)\n    if n == 0:\n        print(f\"   {aid:<3} {name:<26} {'0':<6} {'-':<6} {'-':<7} {'-':<7} {'-':<6} {'-':<5} {len(series)}\")\n        continue\n    mean, med, sd = statistics.mean(series), statistics.median(series), statistics.pstdev(series)\n    p95 = sorted(series)[int(len(series) * 0.95)]\n    print(f\"   {aid:<3} {name:<26} {n:<6} {mean:<6.2f} {med:<7.1f} {sd:<7.2f} \"\n          f\"{sd/mean:<6.2f} {p95:<5} {series.count(0)}\")\n\nh(\"C. HOW BADLY DOES THE BURST POISON A MEAN-BASED BASELINE?\")\nfor label, where in [(\"all data\", \"\"), (\"burst day excluded\", \"AND date(occurred_at)<>'2026-06-03'\")]:\n    counts = dict(q(f\"\"\"SELECT date(occurred_at), COUNT(*) FROM activity_events\n                        WHERE account_id=6 {where} GROUP BY 1\"\"\"))\n    wed = [counts.get(d.isoformat(), 0) for d in ALL_DAYS if d.weekday() == 2]\n    print(f\"   acct 6 Wednesdays, {label:<20} mean={statistics.mean(wed):7.1f}  \"\n          f\"median={statistics.median(wed):6.1f}  n_weeks={len(wed)}\")\nprint(\"   -> the median is unmoved by the burst; the mean is not.\")\n\nh(\"D. THE PRODUCT SCENARIO: last complete week vs a trailing baseline, per location\")\nwk_start, wk_end = last_mon - timedelta(7), last_mon - timedelta(1)\nbase_start = wk_start - timedelta(28)          # 4 prior weeks\nprint(f\"   current week  : {wk_start} .. {wk_end}\")\nprint(f\"   baseline      : {base_start} .. {wk_start - timedelta(1)}  (4 prior weeks, same weekdays)\")\nprint(f\"\\n   {'acct':<5} {'location':<9} {'this wk':<8} {'baseline med':<13} {'delta':<9} note\")\nfor aid in (6, 16, 20):\n    locs = [l for (l,) in q(\"SELECT DISTINCT location FROM activity_events WHERE account_id=? ORDER BY 1\", aid)]\n    if not locs:\n        print(f\"   {aid:<5} {'(none)':<9} {'-':<8} {'-':<13} {'-':<9} account has zero events at all\")\n        continue\n    for loc in locs[:4]:\n        counts = dict(q(\"\"\"SELECT date(occurred_at), COUNT(*) FROM activity_events\n                           WHERE account_id=? AND location=? GROUP BY 1\"\"\", aid, loc))\n        cur = sum(counts.get((wk_start + timedelta(i)).isoformat(), 0) for i in range(7))\n        weeks = [sum(counts.get((base_start + timedelta(w * 7 + i)).isoformat(), 0) for i in range(7))\n                 for w in range(4)]\n        med = statistics.median(weeks)\n        delta = \"n/a\" if med == 0 else f\"{(cur - med) / med:+.0%}\"\n        note = \"baseline is 0 -> % is undefined\" if med == 0 else \"\"\n        print(f\"   {aid:<5} {loc:<9} {cur:<8} {med:<13.1f} {delta:<9} {note}\")\n\nh(\"E. DUPLICATES: do they land inside the window that matters?\")\nprint(\"   dup rows by month:\")\nfor r in q(\"\"\"SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', occurred_at) m, COUNT(*)-COUNT(DISTINCT id) FROM (\n                SELECT id, occurred_at, account_id, location, event_type,\n                       COALESCE(duration_seconds,-1) d, COALESCE(outcome,'~') o\n                FROM activity_events) GROUP BY m\"\"\"):\n    pass  # placeholder, real calc below\ndups = q(\"\"\"SELECT account_id, location, event_type, occurred_at, COUNT(*) n\n            FROM activity_events GROUP BY 1,2,3,4,\n                 COALESCE(duration_seconds,-1), COALESCE(outcome,'~')\n            HAVING n>1 ORDER BY occurred_at\"\"\")\nfor d in dups:\n    print(f\"     acct {d[0]:<3} {d[3]}  {d[2]}\")\nprint(f\"   total duplicate groups: {len(dups)}  (all are exact value-matches with distinct ids)\")\n"
}

```json
{
  "file_path": "c:\\repos\\personal\\qualitara\\home-challenge\\analysis\\README.md",
  "content": "# Seed data profiling\n\nThrowaway exploration scripts, committed as evidence rather than as product code.\nThey run the seed data through SQLite in memory and check it against the anomalies\ndeclared in `seed/generate_seed.py`, so the plan rests on measured facts instead of\non trusting the generator.\n\n```powershell\npython -m pip install tzdata     # Windows has no IANA database\npython analysis/01_profile_seed.py\npython analysis/02_profile_seed_followup.py\n```\n\n`01` establishes shape and confirms each planted anomaly. `02` fixes a methodological\nbug in `01` (coefficient of variation was computed with the mean over all 177 days but\nthe variance over only the days that had events) and tests two design hypotheses:\nwhether a mean-based baseline is poisoned by the burst day, and what the real\nlast-complete-week comparison looks like per location.\n\n## What was measured\n\n| Fact | Value |\n|---|---|\n| Accounts / events | 20 / 12,626 |\n| Range (UTC) | 2026-02-01 10:57 → 2026-07-27 22:20 (177 days) |\n| Last day of data | **Monday 2026-07-27** — last complete Mon–Sun week is 2026-07-20..26 |\n| Account with zero events | 20, Quiet Harbor Spa |\n| Exact duplicates | 12 groups, 12 extra rows, spread Feb–Jul; none inside 2026-07-20..26 |\n| Burst | Account 6, Wed 2026-06-03, **805 events vs a typical 10.9/day — 74×** |\n| NULL `duration_seconds` | 4.0% of calls (and 100% of non-calls, by definition) |\n| NULL `outcome` | 3.1–3.3% across all three event types |\n| Weekend effect | Sat/Sun ≈ 22–23 events/day vs ≈ 85 on weekdays |\n| Local-day shift | 67 of 12,626 events (0.53%) fall on a different calendar day in account-local time |\n| Daily coefficient of variation | 0.62–0.93 for normal accounts; **4.01 for account 6** |\n| Distinct location strings | 15 — `'Site A'` appears in 19 different accounts |\n| Integrity | no orphans, no negative durations, no events before `created_at`, no duration on non-calls |\n\n## What this forces into the design\n\n1. **Median, not mean, for the baseline.** Account 6's Wednesday baseline is a mean of\n   44.6 with the burst included and 12.4 without it. The median is 13.0 either way. One\n   outlier day moves a mean-based \"normal\" by 3.6×; it does not move the median at all.\n2. **Compare like weekdays.** Weekends run at roughly a quarter of weekday volume, so a\n   flat trailing average marks every Saturday as a collapse.\n3. **Gaps are data.** Old Town Barbers has 66 days with zero events out of 177, Willow\n   Creek 60. `GROUP BY date` only returns days that have rows, so any window arithmetic\n   has to fill missing days with zero rather than skip them.\n4. **Small numbers need a confidence signal.** Daily CV reaches 0.93 on the smallest\n   accounts. A per-location weekly count of 5 moving to 9 is \"+64%\" and is also well\n   inside noise. The UI has to show the typical range, not just a percentage.\n5. **`location` is only unique within an account.** Every grouping and filter must be\n   scoped by `account_id`.\n6. **\"Now\" cannot be `GETDATE()`.** The data ends 2026-07-27; today is later than that.\n   Anchoring to the real clock renders an empty dashboard.\n"
}
```

  "file_path": "c:\\repos\\personal\\qualitara\\home-challenge\\ai-log\\decisions\\02-profiling-the-seed-data.md",
  "content": "# 02 — Profiling the seed data before planning\n\n**When:** 2026-08-13, still before `PLAN.md`\n**Agent:** Claude Code (Claude Opus 5, 1M context)\n**Artifacts:** `analysis/01_profile_seed.py`, `analysis/02_profile_seed_followup.py`, `analysis/README.md`\n\n## Why this came before the plan\n\nThe ticket asks what \"normal\" means. That is a data question before it is a product\nquestion — the answer depends on how noisy this data actually is. Planning first and\nprofiling second would have meant designing a baseline against an imagined dataset.\n\n## What I directed the agent to do\n\nThe starter ships `seed/generate_seed.py`, the deterministic generator. Reading it\ngives the planted anomalies for free: a zero-event account, a burst day, 12 exact\nduplicates, planted NULL rates, weekend suppression. I had the agent read it.\n\nThen I made it verify every claim against the actual rows rather than reporting the\ngenerator's intent as fact. The generator says what was *intended*; the evaluation is\nagainst `seed.sql`. All six claims held, and the measured magnitudes turned out to\nmatter more than the claims themselves — 805 events on the burst day against a typical\n10.9, a 74× spike, is a very different design input than \"there is a burst.\"\n\n## Where I overrode the agent\n\n- **It overstated the timezone finding.** It called account-local day handling\n  \"probably the single biggest differentiator in the exercise\" before measuring it.\n  Measured: 67 of 12,626 events (0.53%) land on a different local day. Real, worth\n  getting right, not worth reprioritising the plan around. I had it correct the claim\n  and I am not budgeting the day around timezone work.\n- **Its first profiling script had a statistics bug.** It computed each account's\n  coefficient of variation using a mean over all 177 days but a variance over only the\n  days that had events — inconsistent denominators, and worst exactly on the sparse\n  accounts the metric was meant to describe. The agent caught this itself on review and\n  flagged it; the corrected numbers are in `02_profile_seed_followup.py`. Recording it\n  because the first version looked completely plausible and I would have quoted those\n  CVs in the plan.\n\n## The finding that changed the design\n\nAccount 6's Wednesday baseline:\n\n| | mean | median |\n|---|---|---|\n| all data | 44.6 | 13.0 |\n| burst day excluded | 12.4 | 13.0 |\n\nA single planted outlier moves a mean-based \"normal\" by 3.6×. It does not move the\nmedian at all. That is the whole argument for a median baseline, it is measurable, and\nit is explainable to a customer admin without the word \"model\". This is now the\nload-bearing decision in `PLAN.md`.\n\n## Second finding: the dataset is aligned to the ticket\n\nData ends **Monday 2026-07-27**. The ticket says a customer admin \"should be able to\nlook at this Monday morning and act on it\". The last complete Mon–Sun week is\n2026-07-20..26. The dataset was built for that exact scenario, so the default view\nanchors there rather than to the system clock.\n\n## Cost\n\n~45 minutes. Bought the two decisions above plus a written list of the edge cases the\ntests have to cover, which is most of the value of the profiling.\n"
}

## ASSISTANT  <sub>08/13/2026 23:00:49</sub>

Excluida y reexportada. Nota: el hecho de que existió queda visible en el script y en el mensaje del commit — el contenido está fuera, pero no fingí que no ocurrió. Si prefieres que sea completamente silencioso, dímelo y lo cambio.

---

## Hallazgos verificados contra `seed.sql`

Los seis anomalías declaradas se confirmaron. Las magnitudes son lo interesante:

**El hallazgo que define el diseño** — baseline de miércoles de la cuenta 6:

| | media | mediana |
|---|---|---|
| con todos los datos | **44.6** | 13.0 |
| excluyendo el día burst | 12.4 | **13.0** |

Un solo día atípico mueve una "normalidad" basada en media **3,6×**. A la mediana no la mueve nada. Ese es el argumento completo para un baseline por mediana: medible, defendible, y explicable a un cliente sin decir "modelo".

**El dataset está alineado al ticket.** Termina el **lunes 2026-07-27**. El ticket dice *"un admin debería poder mirar esto un lunes por la mañana"*. La última semana completa es **2026-07-20 a 07-26**. Está construido para ese escenario exacto.

**Lo demás que restringe el diseño:**

- Fin de semana ≈ 22–23 eventos/día vs ≈ 85 entre semana → comparar contra promedio plano marca cada sábado como caída.
- Cuentas dispersas: Old Town Barbers tiene **66 días de 177 sin ningún evento**. `GROUP BY date` solo devuelve días con filas → hay que rellenar huecos con cero, no saltarlos.
- CV diario 0,62–0,93 en cuentas normales. Una sede que pasa de 5 a 9 eventos/semana es "+64%" y a la vez está dentro del ruido → hace falta mostrar el rango típico, no solo el porcentaje.
- Solo **15 strings distintos** de `location`; `'Site A'` aparece en 19 cuentas → todo agrupado y filtrado debe ir scoped por `account_id`.
- Los 12 duplicados están repartidos de febrero a julio y **ninguno cae en la semana final** → deduplicar es higiene, no cambia la demo.
- Fuera de eso los datos están limpios: sin huérfanos, sin duraciones negativas, sin eventos previos al `created_at`.

---

Con esto ya se puede escribir el `PLAN.md`. Dos decisiones que son tuyas antes de que lo redacte:

  "content": "# PLAN — DASH-247\n\nWritten 2026-08-13, before implementation. Left as-written on purpose; where the build\ndiverges from this, the README says so rather than this file being edited to match.\n\nProfiling came first (`analysis/`), because \"what is normal\" is a data question before\nit is a product question. The numbers cited below are measured, not assumed.\n\n---\n\n## 1. What I think the ticket is asking for\n\nThe customer question is *\"is this number normal **for us**?\"* — self-relative, not\nrelative to an industry benchmark. Nobody asked to be compared to other Relay customers,\nand the data has no peer-group framing (20 accounts across 8 industries, 1–15 locations\neach — not enough to benchmark against anyway).\n\nSecond, from product: *\"a customer admin should be able to look at this Monday morning\nand act on it.\"* That is a strong hint about the shape. It means:\n\n- a **weekly** cadence, not daily and not monthly\n- **the week that just ended**, not week-to-date — on Monday morning you review what\n  just happened\n- it has to end in an **action**, which for a multi-location customer means \"go look at\n  Site D\", not \"your numbers are down 6%\"\n\nThird: *\"multi-location customers matter\"* appears in the ticket, and the support pain\nin the background doc is specifically *\"struggle to spot which location needs attention.\"*\nTwo independent mentions. A pure account-level roll-up would fail the ticket.\n\nSo: **the weekly pulse.** One view. For the last complete Mon–Sun week, show the account\ntotal against its own recent norm, then every location ranked by how far it deviates from\n*its own* norm. The answer to \"is this normal\" is a status, not a number, and the answer\nto \"what needs attention\" is the top of the list.\n\n### Explicitly not building\n\n- **Alerting/notifications, ML/forecasting** — out of scope per the ticket.\n- **Peer/industry benchmarking** — \"normal for us\" is self-relative; see above.\n- **Trend charts over time.** Tempting and pretty, but a chart shows *change*, not\n  *normality*, and answering \"is this normal\" from a line chart is exactly the work the\n  customer is currently failing to do. Deferred, noted in README.\n- **Drill-down into individual events.** The ticket ends at \"act on it\"; the acting\n  happens outside the dashboard.\n- **Auth**, per the brief. Account is chosen from a dropdown, standing in for \"the\n  account this admin belongs to\".\n\n---\n\n## 2. What \"normal\" means, concretely\n\nFor a given location (or the account as a whole):\n\n```\ncurrent      = total events in the last complete Mon–Sun week, account-local time\nbaseline     = MEDIAN of the totals of the N complete weeks immediately before it\nspread       = MAD (median absolute deviation) of those same N weekly totals\ntypical band = baseline ± 2 * (MAD / 0.6745)      # robust ~95% band\nstatus       = below | normal | above, by where `current` falls in the band\n```\n\nN defaults to 8 and is user-selectable (4 / 8 / 12). That selector doubles as the\nrequired reload-persistent user input.\n\n### Why median and MAD, and not mean and stdev\n\nThis is the measured reason, not a stylistic preference. Account 6 (Metro Collision) has\na planted burst: **805 events on Wed 2026-06-03 against a typical 10.9/day — 74×**. Its\nWednesday baseline:\n\n| | mean | median |\n|---|---|---|\n| all data | 44.6 | 13.0 |\n| burst day excluded | 12.4 | 13.0 |\n\nOne outlier day moves a mean-based \"normal\" by 3.6×. It leaves the median untouched. A\ncustomer whose baseline silently absorbed a one-off spike would be told that six normal\nweeks in a row were catastrophic. Median + MAD gets this right without special-casing the\nburst, without excluding data, and without anything the ticket calls out of scope.\n\n### Why complete weeks, not rolling windows\n\nA complete Mon–Sun week contains exactly one of each weekday, so day-of-week seasonality\ncancels out of a week-over-week comparison for free. That matters here: measured weekend\nvolume is ~22–23 events/day against ~85 on weekdays. Any partial-week or \"last 7 days\nvs. daily average\" framing would have to correct for that explicitly. Comparing whole\nweeks avoids the problem rather than solving it, which is the cheaper correct answer.\n\nCost of this choice: on Monday the freshest data is up to 7 days old. Accepted — it\nmatches the stated Monday-morning workflow.\n\n### Honesty guards (these are the point, not the polish)\n\nReal accounts in this data break the naive version of the formula:\n\n| Situation | Measured in seed | Behaviour |\n|---|---|---|\n| Account with no events at all | Account 20, Quiet Harbor Spa, 0 rows | Empty state: \"no activity recorded\", no percentages |\n| Fewer than 4 prior complete weeks of history | possible near range start | `insufficient_history`, show counts only |\n| Baseline median is 0 | sparse locations | No percentage — % change from zero is undefined, not infinite |\n| Baseline median < 5 events/week | Old Town Barbers ~6.6/wk account-wide | `low_volume`: show counts and band, suppress the status badge. A location going 5 → 9 is \"+64%\" and is also pure noise |\n| MAD = 0 (identical recent weeks) | likely on small locations | Fall back to a `sqrt(baseline)` spread (count data ≈ Poisson) rather than a zero-width band that flags everything |\n| Days with no events | 66 of 177 days for account 16 | Fill gaps with 0. `GROUP BY date` returns only days that have rows — the single easiest way to get this silently wrong |\n\n---\n\n## 3. Data handling decisions\n\n**Duplicates.** 12 exact value-matches with distinct ids (same account, location, type,\ntimestamp to the second, duration, outcome). Two identical calls at the same location in\nthe same second is a double-write, not two calls. **Deduplicate** on the full value tuple,\nkeeping `MIN(id)`, and surface the excluded count in the API response so it is visible\nrather than silent. Assumption, flagged below. (None of the 12 fall inside the default\ndisplay week, so this is hygiene, not a demo-changer.)\n\n**The burst day stays in the data.** It is real recorded activity; suppressing rows would\nbe inventing a cleaner dataset. The median baseline is what makes it harmless.\n\n**NULLs.** `outcome` is null on ~3.2% of rows, `duration_seconds` on 4.0% of calls (and\non 100% of non-calls by definition). The pulse counts events, so neither affects the\nheadline. Relevant only if a later cut slices by outcome — noted, not built.\n\n**Timezones.** `occurred_at` is UTC; each account has an IANA zone. Measured impact:\n**67 of 12,626 events (0.53%)** land on a different calendar day in account-local time.\nSmall, but it is the difference between a correct daily bucket and a wrong one, and the\nrange spans a DST transition so a fixed offset will not do.\n\nThe wrinkle: **SQL Server's `AT TIME ZONE` takes Windows zone ids, not IANA**. Options\nwere (a) map IANA→Windows in SQL, (b) bucket in C# and give up SQL aggregation, or\n(c) compute the local date once at load time and index it. Going with **(c)**: the load\nstep computes `occurred_local_date` and `local_week_start` in C# via `TimeZoneInfo`\n(IANA-aware on .NET 6+), and every aggregate groups on those columns. Aggregation stays\nin SQL, the DST edge is handled by a real tz database, and the query stays sargable.\nTrade-off: denormalized, and would need a backfill if an account's timezone changed. For\nthis dataset, correct and cheap.\n\n**\"Now\" is not the system clock.** Data ends 2026-07-27; today is later. The default week\nis derived from `MAX(occurred_at)`, not `GETUTCDATE()`. Anchoring to the real clock would\nrender an empty dashboard — the kind of thing that looks like a broken build.\n\n**Seeding must be idempotent.** The provided `seed.sql` is ~12.6k bare `INSERT`s with\nexplicit ids; running it twice violates the primary key. It gets wrapped so re-running\nmigrations is safe (guard on a marker row / `NOT EXISTS` per batch). The raw file stays\nunmodified in the repo — the brief says treat it as production data.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Shape of the build\n\n**DB — SQL Server 2022 in Docker.** It is what the team runs. `docker compose up` plus a\nconnection string; no manual setup steps in the README beyond that.\n\n**Backend — .NET 8 minimal API.**\n- **EF Core for migrations and the accounts read** — standard tooling, and the brief asks\n  for the stack's normal migration story.\n- **Dapper for the analytics query** — the pulse is a windowed aggregate with gap filling.\n  Expressing that through LINQ would be fighting the ORM to produce SQL I would rather\n  write directly and be able to read. Documented in the README as a deliberate split.\n\nOne endpoint does the real work:\n\n```\nGET /api/accounts/{accountId}/weekly-pulse\n      ?weekStart=2026-07-20        # default: last complete week in the data\n      &baselineWeeks=8             # 4 | 8 | 12\n      &eventType=all               # all | call_received | lead_created | appointment_set\n```\n\nReturns the account roll-up, the per-location rows sorted by absolute deviation, the\nresolved window boundaries, and a `dataQuality` block (duplicates excluded, whether the\naccount has any data at all). Supporting: `GET /api/accounts` for the selector.\n\nThe aggregation is a single query: dedup → local-date bucket → weekly totals per location\nacross the current + N baseline weeks → gap-fill → median/MAD per location. Median and MAD\nin T-SQL via `PERCENTILE_CONT`.\n\n**Frontend — Angular 18 standalone + signals.** One route, one view: account selector,\nweek selector, baseline-window selector, event-type filter. **All four live in the URL\nquery string**, so state survives reload and the view is shareable — a customer admin\npasting a link to their account manager is the actual use case, which `localStorage` would\nnot serve. No NgRx; four params and one HTTP call do not need a store, and I would rather\nspend the budget on the aggregate being right.\n\nPresentation is deliberately plain: a status line for the account, a table for locations\nwith current / typical band / delta / status. Function over form, per the brief.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Tests\n\nAiming at the parts that can be wrong in ways that matter, using the profiled numbers as\ngolden values.\n\n**Unit — the baseline calculator** (no DB):\n- burst immunity: a series with one 74× day yields the same median baseline as without it\n- gap filling: missing days count as 0, not as absent\n- `baseline = 0` → no percentage, status is not \"down 100%\"\n- fewer than 4 prior weeks → `insufficient_history`\n- `MAD = 0` → falls back to the sqrt spread, does not divide by zero\n- low volume → status suppressed\n- band arithmetic at the boundary (current exactly on the edge)\n\n**Integration — against the seeded DB**, asserting numbers verified in `analysis/`:\n- total rows 12,626; accounts 20\n- account 6 on 2026-06-03 = 805 events\n- account 20 returns an empty-state payload, not a 500 and not a NaN\n- dedup removes exactly 12 rows\n- default week resolves to 2026-07-20..26\n- a known location's weekly total matches a hand-computed value\n\n**Frontend**: one test that query params round-trip through a reload.\n\nNot chasing coverage. If something has to give, the integration aggregates stay.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Open questions\n\nNot sending these to the recruiter — the assumptions are defensible and waiting would\ncost more than being wrong here would. Recording both halves per the brief.\n\n1. **Is \"normal\" self-relative or peer-relative?** → Assuming self-relative. The customer\n   phrasing is \"normal *for us*\", and 20 accounts is not a benchmark set.\n2. **Last complete week, or week-to-date?** → Last complete week. Matches the\n   Monday-morning framing; week-to-date needs a partial-week correction that adds error\n   for no clear gain.\n3. **Are exact duplicate events real, or ingestion artifacts?** → Assuming artifacts and\n   deduplicating, but reporting the count so the assumption is visible in the response.\n4. **Should a spike like account 6's be excluded from history, or is it real?** → Leaving\n   it in and letting the robust statistic absorb it. Deleting real activity to make a\n   metric behave is the worse failure.\n5. **Is \"activity\" all three event types together?** → Yes by default, with a filter.\n   Calls, leads and appointments are different funnel stages and a customer may well want\n   them separately, but summing them answers \"is this normal\" at a glance.\n6. **What does a brand-new account see?** → `insufficient_history` until 4 complete weeks\n   exist. `PLAN.md` commiteado en `9e64563`, **antes de una sola línea de código**. El historial ya cuenta la historia correcta: log → datos → perfilado → plan → (código).

**Una corrección a lo que te dije antes.** Te dije que el baseline tenía que comparar *mismo día de la semana*. Al diseñarlo me di cuenta de que es innecesario: una semana Lun–Dom completa contiene exactamente un día de cada tipo, así que la estacionalidad de fin de semana **se cancela sola** al comparar semanas completas. Mismo resultado, la mitad de complejidad. El hallazgo del fin de semana sigue siendo relevante — pero como justificación de *por qué* comparamos semanas enteras, no como mecánica a implementar.

**Lo esencial del plan:**

| Decisión | Razón medida |
|---|---|
| Mediana + MAD sobre N semanas previas | La media del baseline de la cuenta 6 pasa de 12.4 → 44.6 por un solo día; la mediana no se mueve de 13.0 |
| Semanas completas Lun–Dom | La estacionalidad semanal se cancela por construcción |
| `occurred_local_date` calculada en C# al cargar | `AT TIME ZONE` de SQL Server no acepta IANA, y el rango cruza un cambio de horario |
| "Ahora" = `MAX(occurred_at)` | Los datos terminan 2026-07-27; con reloj real el dashboard sale vacío |
| EF Core migraciones + Dapper para el agregado | La consulta tiene ventanas y relleno de huecos; en LINQ sería pelear con el ORM |
| Estado en query params, no localStorage | Sobrevive al reload **y** el link es compartible con el account manager |

Y seis guardas que son el verdadero contenido: cuenta sin eventos, baseline en cero, historia insuficiente, MAD=0, volumen bajo (suprime el badge — pasar de 5 a 9 es "+64%" y es ruido puro), y relleno de días faltantes.

Las **6 preguntas abiertas** quedaron documentadas con el supuesto tomado en cada una, que es exactamente lo que el brief pide en lugar de adivinar en silencio.

Genial, tenemos un plan inicial muy bueno. Aqui van algunas cosas que analicé luego de leer el plan y que necesito que me ayudes a corroborar.

Antes de codear: el perfilado midió historia y días-en-cero por cuenta, no por sede, pero el plan define insufficient_history y low_volume a nivel de sede. Mide, por (account_id, location): cuántas semanas completas de historia tiene, cuántas tienen mediana semanal de baseline = 0, y cuántas caen bajo el umbral de low_volume (<5 eventos/semana). Dime cuántas sedes activan cada rama. Si alguna rama no la dispara ninguna sede del dataset, dímelo — significa que la voy a testear con fixtures sintéticos, no con datos reales, y quiero saberlo antes de escribir los tests.

Y por separado, el override técnico que te mencioné, también sin saltos internos:

Encontré una contradicción en el PLAN.md que quiero resolver antes de implementar. La sección 4 dice que la agregación es un solo query con median y MAD en T-SQL vía PERCENTILE_CONT. La sección 5 promete unit tests del baseline calculator sin base de datos: inmunidad al burst, gap filling, baseline=0, MAD=0 con fallback sqrt. Si la mediana y el MAD viven en T-SQL, ese calculator no existe y esos siete unit tests no se pueden escribir. Además PERCENTILE_CONT en SQL Server es window function, no agrega por GROUP BY, y para MAD necesitaría dos pasadas.

Mi decisión: partir la responsabilidad. SQL agrega hasta totales semanales por (location, week) rellenando huecos con cero — eso sigue siendo agregación real sobre 12.6k filas, que es lo que el brief exige. C# calcula mediana, MAD, banda y estado sobre ese resultado, que son unas 200 filas como máximo. Gano unit tests sin BD, evito PERCENTILE_CONT, y a esa escala no pierdo nada de rendimiento.

Anexa esto al final de PLAN.md como Amendment con la hora, sin editar el cuerpo original, y commitéalo aparte antes de empezar el código.

| Rama | Sedes que la disparan | Veredicto |
|---|---|---|
| `insufficient_history` | **0** — todas tienen 24–25 semanas | ❌ **fixture sintético** |
| `baseline_zero` | **0** — la mediana mínima del dataset es **3,5** | ❌ **fixture sintético** |
| `low_volume` (<5/sem) | **8** (12%) | ✅ datos reales |
| `mad_zero` | **1** | ✅ datos reales |

Barriendo las **18 semanas ancla válidas** (1.242 evaluaciones), `low_volume` toca 24 sedes y `mad_zero` 6. Las otras dos **nunca se disparan**: ninguna sede del dataset tiene menos de 24 semanas de historia ni una mediana de baseline en cero. La cuenta vacía (id 20) sí es real y cubre su propia rama por separado.

**Dos hallazgos que no esperaba y que valen más que el conteo:**

**1. El baseline por defecto de la cuenta 6 contiene la semana del burst.** Mira Site J:

```
baseline = [4, 56, 11, 2, 3, 3, 5, 3]     current = 5
           mediana = 3.5   →  +43% (por encima de lo normal)
           media   = 10.9  →  −54% (colapso)
```

La pantalla por defecto **demuestra la inmunidad al burst con datos reales**, no con un fixture. Siete de las ocho sedes `low_volume` son de la cuenta 6 y todas tienen ese 50-70 incrustado en el baseline. Es el mejor caso de prueba del dataset y estaba escondido.

**2. `MAD = 0` no significa "semanas idénticas".** El caso real es cuenta 12 / Site A:

```
baseline = [8, 8, 8, 8, 3, 6, 8, 14]   mediana = 8   MAD = 0
```

Hay variación real (3 y 14), pero como *más de la mitad* de las semanas valen exactamente 8, el MAD colapsa a cero. Mi redacción en el `PLAN.md` ("identical recent weeks") es imprecisa — la condición es mayoría idéntica, no todas. Sin el fallback, esa sede marcaría 3 y 14 como anomalías extremas.

Ahora el amendment:

## ASSISTANT  <sub>08/14/2026 00:03:56</sub>

Ninguna de las siete sobra — todas son reglas que un agente rompe por defecto. Pero faltan varias, y las primeras tres las violaría casi con certeza.

**Alta prioridad (violación casi segura):**

1. **Rellenar huecos con cero es obligatorio.** `GROUP BY` solo devuelve filas que existen. Un agente escribiendo el SQL va a producir una consulta que *salta* las semanas sin eventos, y el baseline queda calculado sobre 5 valores en vez de 8 — silenciosamente. Con 66 días en cero en la cuenta 16, esto no es teórico. Es el bug más probable de todo el proyecto.

2. **`AT TIME ZONE` de SQL Server no acepta IANA.** El agente va a escribir `AT TIME ZONE 'America/Chicago'` porque es lo natural, y va a fallar. Peor: si le decimos solo "usa `occurred_local_date`", en cuanto toque esa query va a intentar "arreglarla" moviendo la conversión a SQL. Hay que dejar escrito el *por qué*, no solo el *qué*.

3. **Deduplicar por tupla completa quedándose con `MIN(id)`, y exponer el conteo.** El agente va a consultar `activity_events` directo. Los 12 duplicados entran al baseline sin que nadie se entere.

**Media prioridad:**

4. **El día burst (2026-06-03) se queda en los datos.** Cuando un agente ve 805 eventos contra 10,9 típicos, su instinto es filtrarlo como dato sucio. Todo el diseño depende de que *no* se filtre — la mediana es lo que lo absorbe.

5. **Estado de UI en query params de la URL, no en un servicio ni localStorage.** Angular por defecto lo mete en un servicio con signals y se pierde al recargar. Es requisito duro del brief.

6. **EF Core solo para migraciones y el read de `accounts`; el agregado va en Dapper.** Si no está escrito, el agente convierte la query a LINQ "para ser consistente".

7. **Baseline en cero → no hay porcentaje.** Va a dividir y va a emitir `Infinity` o `-100%`. Se puede plegar dentro de una línea de guardas junto con `low_volume` suprimiendo el badge.

**Sobre las tuyas:** solo un ajuste de forma — la 5 y la 6 son ambas de tests, las agruparía en un bloque. Y la 4 la afinaría a *"no se modifica; si hace falta idempotencia, se envuelve"*, porque el agente va a querer editar `seed.sql` para meterle `IF NOT EXISTS` en vez de envolverlo.

Eso da 13 reglas. Entran en 40 líneas si van agrupadas en seis bloques (Datos / Agregación / Tiempo / Tests / Frontend / Alcance) con una línea por regla y cero prosa.

¿Escribo con las 13, o quieres podar algunas?

See PLAN Amendment 2.\n- Missing weeks and days materialise as `0`. `GROUP BY` returns only rows that exist; a\n  baseline averaged over 5 of 8 weeks is wrong and looks fine.\n- Baseline median of 0 → no percentage at all. Never emit `Infinity`, `NaN` or `-100%`.\n  `low_volume` (median < 5/week) shows counts and band but suppresses the status badge.\n\n## Time\n- Weeks are Monday–Sunday in the **account's local time**, bucketed on `occurred_local_date`.\n- `occurred_local_date` is precomputed at load time in C# (`TimeZoneInfo`, IANA-aware) and\n  indexed — it keeps the day bucket sargable and the conversion unit-testable without a\n  database. Do not move the conversion into the query.\n- \"Now\" is `MAX(occurred_at)`, never `GETUTCDATE()` or `DateTime.Now`. The data ends\n  2026-07-27; the system clock renders an empty dashboard.\n\n## Tests\n- Golden numbers come from `analysis/`, never from intuition. Needing a new expected value\n  means measuring it there first.\n- `insufficient_history` and `baseline_zero` have no real data behind them in this seed\n  (minimum baseline median is 3.5, minimum history 24 weeks). Test them with synthetic\n  fixtures — do not invent seed rows to reach them.\n\n## Stack\n- EF Core owns migrations and the `accounts` read. The pulse aggregate is Dapper. Do not\n  \"unify\" it back into LINQ.\n- All UI state — account, week, baseline window, event type — lives in URL query params. Not\n  a service field, not `localStorage`. It must survive reload and be shareable by link.\n\n## Out of scope\n- Auth, alerting/notifications, ML/forecasting, trend charts, per-event drill-down. Do not add\n  them, and do not scaffold them \"for later\".\n"
}

```
sys.time_zone_info           → 141 zonas, todas con nombres Windows
                               ('Central Standard Time', 'US Mountain Standard Time'…)

AT TIME ZONE 'America/Phoenix'        → FAILED: invalid time zone    ❌

El contenedor Linux **no** lee la base de zonas del SO: SQL Server trae su propio catálogo de 141 zonas con nomenclatura Windows en ambas plataformas. La justificación del `PLAN.md` era correcta. EF Core solo: migraciones y entidades como ya estaba, y la query analítica con SqlQueryRaw parametrizado mapeando a un tipo plano, no a una entidad.

Y algo que quiero que hagamos aparte: el SQL de la agregación va en su propio archivo .sql como embedded resource, no como string embebido en el C#. Así queda legible, se puede diffear en un PR y podemos correrlo directo contra la base para verificarlo.

Anexa esto como Amendment 4 antes de codear, con el razonamiento de por qué descartamos el split. Ya que el punto 4 del plan dice Dapper, quiero que quede el rastro de por qué cambió.

It is not a claim about every SQL Server build.\n\n## Amendment 4 — 2026-08-13 19:47 (UTC-05:00): drop Dapper; SQL moves to its own file\n\n**Trigger.** §4 puts Dapper in the stack for the analytics read, with EF Core owning\nmigrations and entities. Re-examined before writing any of it: the reason §4 wanted\nDapper was \"the pulse is a windowed aggregate with gap filling; expressing that through\nLINQ would be fighting the ORM\". That reason is real, but it argues for **raw SQL**, not\nfor Dapper specifically — and EF Core already runs raw SQL.\n\n**Decision — EF Core only.**\n\n- Migrations and entities: unchanged, EF Core as planned.\n- The pulse aggregate: `Database.SqlQueryRaw<T>(...)`, parameterised, projecting onto a\n  **flat DTO, not an entity**. EF Core 8 supports unmapped result types for exactly this,\n  the SQL is passed through verbatim, and results are not change-tracked.\n\n**Why dropping it is right rather than merely lighter:**\n\n- It buys nothing here. The concern §4 raised was LINQ mangling the aggregate;\n  `SqlQueryRaw` is hand-written SQL, so that concern is answered without a second\n  library. Dapper's advantage over EF is a thinner mapper — irrelevant when the result is\n  one flat DTO of primitives.\n- Two data-access stacks means two connection stories, two configuration points, two\n  transaction models and two things a reviewer has to hold in their head to run this. In\n  a 4–6 hour slice that is cost with no matching benefit.\n- One less package to justify in the README.\n\n**Separately: the aggregation SQL lives in its own `.sql` file, embedded as a resource** —\nnot a C# string literal, verbatim or otherwise. Reasons, in order of how much they matter:\n\n1. It can be run **directly against the database** to verify the numbers, which is the\n   whole point given that correct aggregates are the thing being evaluated. A query\n   trapped inside a string literal can only be tested through the app.\n2. It diffs as SQL in a pull request instead of as a wall of escaped text.\n3. Syntax highlighting and formatting survive, so the query stays readable at the length\n   this one will reach.\n4. No interpolation is possible, which forces parameterisation rather than relying on\n   discipline.\n\n**Risks accepted.** `SqlQueryRaw<T>` for unmapped types needs EF Core 8+ (we are on 8), and\nit binds by **column name to property name** — a renamed column in the `.sql` silently\nproduces a mapping failure rather than a compile error. Mitigation is the integration test\nasserting golden numbers: it fails immediately if the mapping breaks. Embedded resource\nnames are also path-derived and easy to typo, so the loader resolves the resource once at\nstartup and throws with the list of available names if it is missing, rather than failing\nlater at first request."
}
